[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.2, F20.0, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because… it’s been bothering me, and my wife said I ought to talk to somebody, though she doesn’t see it the way I do. When I’m sitting with the men at the domino table, you know, just regular talk going on, sometimes I can tell there are little messages in what they’re saying. Not loud, not like they come right out and say my name, but the way they say certain things, or repeat something, it feels meant for me.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s the radio announcer too. I’ll be listening, minding my own business, and there’ll be a phrase or a warning kind of tucked in there, like they’re telling me to be careful. My wife says, “That’s just talk, that’s just the radio,” but I don’t think it’s just nothing. I know when something is meant for me. It’s got me uneasy, and that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear a voice speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do sometimes. Not all day or anything like that, and it’s not usually when there’s a lot going on. It’s more in the afternoon, when the house gets real quiet and I’m sitting there by myself. I’ll hear my old friend call my name, like from out on the porch. Clear enough that I’ll turn my head, because it sounds like he’s standing right there.\n\nOther times it’s not words exactly, it’ll be him humming an old choir song, the kind we used to hear years back. And I know how that sounds, doctor, I know folks might say it’s just my mind or my ears playing tricks, but in the moment it feels real plain to me. Like someone is right there just outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: Well… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. I haven’t had any big fall where I hit my head or anything like that, at least nothing I’d call a head injury. And I haven’t been laid up sick recently either, not in some serious way.\n\nI’m 84, so, you know, there’s always little aches and tired days and such, and sometimes I have to think a minute because things run together when you get older. But as far as some recent illness or bump to the head that started all this, I can’t say there’s been one.\n\nDoctor: How long have these experiences been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about nine months now, give or take. Not every single day, no, but on and off. That’s why at first I didn’t make too much of it, because one week it might happen a few times and then it would quiet down some, and I’d think maybe it was nothing.\n\nBut when I look back on it, the voices and those little messages in things people say or on the radio… that’s been going on close to nine months. Long enough that my wife noticed I was getting bothered by it, and long enough that I can’t really just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. At my age, I suppose I do have those moments where I walk into a room and forget what I went in there for, or I’ll misplace my glasses and then find them somewhere silly. But I can’t say I’ve noticed some big change, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes my wife will say, “You already told me that,” and I’ll laugh it off, because I’ve been telling long stories my whole life, so maybe I just repeat myself. But I still know who people are, I know where I am, I keep up with the day well enough most of the time. So… I’m not sure I’d call it trouble with my memory. Maybe just ordinary old-man forgetfulness, unless you mean something more particular.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed that your memory or thinking has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Well… she hasn’t sat me down and said, “Your memory is worse,” not in so many words. She’ll notice little things, sure — like if I tell her the same story twice, or if I put something down and then fuss around looking for it. But she’s been married to me a long time, so she also knows I’ve always been a bit that way, especially with my things.\n\nAs for my thinking, I don’t know. I suppose she might say I get more stuck on something than I used to, or I take longer to sort things out, but I’m not sure if that’s my memory getting worse or just me being old and tired some days. She hasn’t told me, “This past year you’ve really gone downhill,” nothing like that that I can remember. If you asked her, she might have her own opinion, but from what she’s said to me, I don’t think there’s been some clear big change.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have everyday tasks become harder for you than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Some things have, yes. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always kept myself busy, even after I wasn’t working anymore. But I’ve missed choir rehearsals, and that’s not like me. I used to be steady with that — you know, show up, sit where I sit, sing my part, see the same folks. Lately I’ll think about going and then I just… don’t. Or I get there in my mind, if that makes sense, but not out the door.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I’ve always taken pride in that little patch. Even if it wasn’t perfect, I’d keep after it. But there are parts of it I’ve let go, weeds coming up where I would’ve never tolerated it before. I see it, and I know it needs doing, but it feels harder to get myself to stay with it.\n\nThe domino games are maybe the most embarrassing. I used to enjoy that, sitting with the fellas, joking around. But I guess I’ve made it uncomfortable sometimes, or I feel like I have, and now I don’t join in like I used to. So yes, everyday things haven’t stopped completely, but they’ve gotten harder in those ways.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you ever had anything like this earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No… not really like this, not that I can think of. I mean, I’ve had hard times in my life, like anybody who gets to be my age, but the voices and those special messages — that’s been more recent. On and off, I’d say about nine months now.\n\nIt’s not every minute of every day, but it’s been around enough that I can’t say it’s just one odd afternoon or something I imagined once and forgot about. Before that, I don’t remember having this same kind of thing earlier in my life.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife had to start handling things for you that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly in the sense that she’s taken over all my affairs or anything like that. I still try to do my own things. But I suppose, if I’m being honest, she’s had to do more nudging and reminding than she used to.\n\nLike with choir — I used to keep track of that myself. I knew what night it was, I’d get ready, I’d go. Lately I’ve missed rehearsals, and she’ll say, “Aren’t you supposed to be going tonight?” or she’ll notice when I don’t. And the garden, that was always my thing. She didn’t have to tell me about weeds or which part needed attention, but now there are sections I’ve just let go, and she’ll point it out or try to encourage me to get back to it.\n\nAnd the domino games… she doesn’t handle that for me, exactly, but she knows I don’t go like I used to. I think I’ve made things uncomfortable there, or at least it feels that way, and I’ve pulled back. So I wouldn’t say she’s taken over my life, no. But yes, she’s had to notice more, remind me more, and sort of push me along in places I used to manage without anyone saying a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you been told by a doctor that you have Parkinson’s disease?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe any doctor has told me that. Parkinson’s, you mean like that specific disease? No, I don’t recall ever being given that diagnosis. I’ve seen doctors over the years for this and that, the usual things when you get older, but nobody has sat me down and said, “You have Parkinson’s disease,” not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people don’t seem to see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… no, not really seeing, I don’t think. It’s more hearing, if I’m saying it right.\n\nSome afternoons, when the house is quiet — and it gets very quiet in there, you know, when there’s no television on and my wife is in another room or out doing something — I’ll hear my old friend call my name from the porch. Clear as anything. Not like a memory exactly, not like I’m just thinking of him. It’ll sound like he’s standing out there and saying my name, like he used to.\n\nAnd sometimes I hear him hum one of the choir songs, just a little bit of it, but clear enough that I know the tune. I’ll stop what I’m doing and listen, because for a second it feels so real. Then I’ll look, or I’ll wait, and there’s nobody there. So… seeing, no. But hearing, yes, that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems with your hearing?\n\nPatient: My hearing? I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really thought of myself as hard of hearing, if that’s what you mean. My wife might say I don’t always catch what she says, but sometimes she’s talking from the other room, and I tell her nobody can hear properly through walls. \n\nI don’t wear hearing aids or anything like that. I can usually follow a conversation if someone’s in front of me and speaking clear. In a noisy place, maybe it gets muddled, but I think that happens to a lot of people my age. So… no major problem that I know of. Not something a doctor has told me about, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain condition, like a stroke or dementia?\n\nPatient: A brain condition… no, I don’t think I’ve been told that. Not a stroke, no. Nobody said dementia to me either, at least not in any official way. I’ve had doctors ask me questions, you know, what day it is and who the president is, that kind of thing, but I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying, “You’ve had a stroke,” or “You have dementia.”\n\nAt my age, people forget names or misplace things now and then, and sometimes folks make a big deal out of it, but as far as a diagnosis like that, no. Not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medicines in the past year?\n\nPatient: New medicines… hmm. I’m trying to think back over the year, and that’s harder than it sounds, because the months sort of run together when you’re not working and the routine is mostly the same.\n\nI don’t believe so. Not that I can clearly remember. I take what I’m supposed to take, and my wife helps keep an eye on things, but I don’t recall a doctor saying, “Here, this is a brand-new medicine,” recently. There may have been refills, or the pharmacy giving me bottles that look a little different, but as far as something new started in the past year… I’m not sure. I don’t think so.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Confused or not clearheaded… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF06.2, F20.0, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because… it’s been bothering me, and my wife said I ought to talk to somebody, though she doesn’t see it the way I do. When I’m sitting with the men at the domino table, you know, just regular talk going on, sometimes I can tell there are little messages in what they’re saying. Not loud, not like they come right out and say my name, but the way they say certain things, or repeat something, it feels meant for me.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s the radio announcer too. I’ll be listening, minding my own business, and there’ll be a phrase or a warning kind of tucked in there, like they’re telling me to be careful. My wife says, “That’s just talk, that’s just the radio,” but I don’t think it’s just nothing. I know when something is meant for me. It’s got me uneasy, and that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear a voice speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do sometimes. Not all day or anything like that, and it’s not usually when there’s a lot going on. It’s more in the afternoon, when the house gets real quiet and I’m sitting there by myself. I’ll hear my old friend call my name, like from out on the porch. Clear enough that I’ll turn my head, because it sounds like he’s standing right there.\n\nOther times it’s not words exactly, it’ll be him humming an old choir song, the kind we used to hear years back. And I know how that sounds, doctor, I know folks might say it’s just my mind or my ears playing tricks, but in the moment it feels real plain to me. Like someone is right there just outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: Well… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. I haven’t had any big fall where I hit my head or anything like that, at least nothing I’d call a head injury. And I haven’t been laid up sick recently either, not in some serious way.\n\nI’m 84, so, you know, there’s always little aches and tired days and such, and sometimes I have to think a minute because things run together when you get older. But as far as some recent illness or bump to the head that started all this, I can’t say there’s been one.\n\nDoctor: How long have these experiences been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about nine months now, give or take. Not every single day, no, but on and off. That’s why at first I didn’t make too much of it, because one week it might happen a few times and then it would quiet down some, and I’d think maybe it was nothing.\n\nBut when I look back on it, the voices and those little messages in things people say or on the radio… that’s been going on close to nine months. Long enough that my wife noticed I was getting bothered by it, and long enough that I can’t really just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. At my age, I suppose I do have those moments where I walk into a room and forget what I went in there for, or I’ll misplace my glasses and then find them somewhere silly. But I can’t say I’ve noticed some big change, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes my wife will say, “You already told me that,” and I’ll laugh it off, because I’ve been telling long stories my whole life, so maybe I just repeat myself. But I still know who people are, I know where I am, I keep up with the day well enough most of the time. So… I’m not sure I’d call it trouble with my memory. Maybe just ordinary old-man forgetfulness, unless you mean something more particular.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed that your memory or thinking has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Well… she hasn’t sat me down and said, “Your memory is worse,” not in so many words. She’ll notice little things, sure — like if I tell her the same story twice, or if I put something down and then fuss around looking for it. But she’s been married to me a long time, so she also knows I’ve always been a bit that way, especially with my things.\n\nAs for my thinking, I don’t know. I suppose she might say I get more stuck on something than I used to, or I take longer to sort things out, but I’m not sure if that’s my memory getting worse or just me being old and tired some days. She hasn’t told me, “This past year you’ve really gone downhill,” nothing like that that I can remember. If you asked her, she might have her own opinion, but from what she’s said to me, I don’t think there’s been some clear big change.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have everyday tasks become harder for you than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Some things have, yes. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always kept myself busy, even after I wasn’t working anymore. But I’ve missed choir rehearsals, and that’s not like me. I used to be steady with that — you know, show up, sit where I sit, sing my part, see the same folks. Lately I’ll think about going and then I just… don’t. Or I get there in my mind, if that makes sense, but not out the door.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I’ve always taken pride in that little patch. Even if it wasn’t perfect, I’d keep after it. But there are parts of it I’ve let go, weeds coming up where I would’ve never tolerated it before. I see it, and I know it needs doing, but it feels harder to get myself to stay with it.\n\nThe domino games are maybe the most embarrassing. I used to enjoy that, sitting with the fellas, joking around. But I guess I’ve made it uncomfortable sometimes, or I feel like I have, and now I don’t join in like I used to. So yes, everyday things haven’t stopped completely, but they’ve gotten harder in those ways.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you ever had anything like this earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No… not really like this, not that I can think of. I mean, I’ve had hard times in my life, like anybody who gets to be my age, but the voices and those special messages — that’s been more recent. On and off, I’d say about nine months now.\n\nIt’s not every minute of every day, but it’s been around enough that I can’t say it’s just one odd afternoon or something I imagined once and forgot about. Before that, I don’t remember having this same kind of thing earlier in my life.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife had to start handling things for you that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly in the sense that she’s taken over all my affairs or anything like that. I still try to do my own things. But I suppose, if I’m being honest, she’s had to do more nudging and reminding than she used to.\n\nLike with choir — I used to keep track of that myself. I knew what night it was, I’d get ready, I’d go. Lately I’ve missed rehearsals, and she’ll say, “Aren’t you supposed to be going tonight?” or she’ll notice when I don’t. And the garden, that was always my thing. She didn’t have to tell me about weeds or which part needed attention, but now there are sections I’ve just let go, and she’ll point it out or try to encourage me to get back to it.\n\nAnd the domino games… she doesn’t handle that for me, exactly, but she knows I don’t go like I used to. I think I’ve made things uncomfortable there, or at least it feels that way, and I’ve pulled back. So I wouldn’t say she’s taken over my life, no. But yes, she’s had to notice more, remind me more, and sort of push me along in places I used to manage without anyone saying a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you been told by a doctor that you have Parkinson’s disease?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe any doctor has told me that. Parkinson’s, you mean like that specific disease? No, I don’t recall ever being given that diagnosis. I’ve seen doctors over the years for this and that, the usual things when you get older, but nobody has sat me down and said, “You have Parkinson’s disease,” not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people don’t seem to see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… no, not really seeing, I don’t think. It’s more hearing, if I’m saying it right.\n\nSome afternoons, when the house is quiet — and it gets very quiet in there, you know, when there’s no television on and my wife is in another room or out doing something — I’ll hear my old friend call my name from the porch. Clear as anything. Not like a memory exactly, not like I’m just thinking of him. It’ll sound like he’s standing out there and saying my name, like he used to.\n\nAnd sometimes I hear him hum one of the choir songs, just a little bit of it, but clear enough that I know the tune. I’ll stop what I’m doing and listen, because for a second it feels so real. Then I’ll look, or I’ll wait, and there’s nobody there. So… seeing, no. But hearing, yes, that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems with your hearing?\n\nPatient: My hearing? I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really thought of myself as hard of hearing, if that’s what you mean. My wife might say I don’t always catch what she says, but sometimes she’s talking from the other room, and I tell her nobody can hear properly through walls. \n\nI don’t wear hearing aids or anything like that. I can usually follow a conversation if someone’s in front of me and speaking clear. In a noisy place, maybe it gets muddled, but I think that happens to a lot of people my age. So… no major problem that I know of. Not something a doctor has told me about, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain condition, like a stroke or dementia?\n\nPatient: A brain condition… no, I don’t think I’ve been told that. Not a stroke, no. Nobody said dementia to me either, at least not in any official way. I’ve had doctors ask me questions, you know, what day it is and who the president is, that kind of thing, but I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying, “You’ve had a stroke,” or “You have dementia.”\n\nAt my age, people forget names or misplace things now and then, and sometimes folks make a big deal out of it, but as far as a diagnosis like that, no. Not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medicines in the past year?\n\nPatient: New medicines… hmm. I’m trying to think back over the year, and that’s harder than it sounds, because the months sort of run together when you’re not working and the routine is mostly the same.\n\nI don’t believe so. Not that I can clearly remember. I take what I’m supposed to take, and my wife helps keep an eye on things, but I don’t recall a doctor saying, “Here, this is a brand-new medicine,” recently. There may have been refills, or the pharmacy giving me bottles that look a little different, but as far as something new started in the past year… I’m not sure. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times in the past year when you felt confused or not clearheaded?\n\nPatient: Confused or not clearheaded… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are mornings where I wake up and it takes me a minute to get myself going, remember what day it is or what I was planning to do, but I don’t know if I’d call that confusion. Sometimes if there’s a lot going on, or people are talking fast, my head feels kind of cloudy, like I need folks to slow down.\n\nBut as far as big spells where I didn’t know where I was or couldn’t make sense of things, I can’t really say that’s happened. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. My wife might have her own opinion, because wives notice everything, but from my side, I’d say nothing too clear-cut. More just ordinary getting older and needing a little time to think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I wouldn’t say I stopped managing everything, no. I still try to do my part around the house and keep up with things, but there are some things I’ve let slide, if I’m being honest.

Like choir, for example. I used to be pretty steady about going to rehearsals, and lately I’ve missed some. Sometimes I just don’t get myself together to go, or by the time it’s time to leave, I’ve sort of talked myself out of it. And my garden too — that used to be something I kept up with better. I knew what needed watering, what needed trimming, what weeds to pull. Now there are parts of it I just let go longer than I should, and then it feels like too much to catch back up.

And the domino games… I used to enjoy those. But I think I’ve made them uncomfortable a few times, or maybe I just felt like people weren’t enjoying me being there the same way. So I don’t join like I used to. I still think about going, but then I don’t. So yes, I guess some of my regular responsibilities and activities, I haven’t been keeping up with them the way I used to.

I’ll be listening, minding my own business, and there’ll be a phrase or a warning kind of tucked in there, like they’re telling me to be careful. My wife says, “That’s just talk, that’s just the radio,” but I don’t think it’s just nothing. I know when something is meant for me. It’s got me uneasy, and that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear a voice speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do sometimes. Not all day or anything like that, and it’s not usually when there’s a lot going on. It’s more in the afternoon, when the house gets real quiet and I’m sitting there by myself. I’ll hear my old friend call my name, like from out on the porch. Clear enough that I’ll turn my head, because it sounds like he’s standing right there.\n\nOther times it’s not words exactly, it’ll be him humming an old choir song, the kind we used to hear years back. And I know how that sounds, doctor, I know folks might say it’s just my mind or my ears playing tricks, but in the moment it feels real plain to me. Like someone is right there just outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: Well… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. I haven’t had any big fall where I hit my head or anything like that, at least nothing I’d call a head injury. And I haven’t been laid up sick recently either, not in some serious way.\n\nI’m 84, so, you know, there’s always little aches and tired days and such, and sometimes I have to think a minute because things run together when you get older. But as far as some recent illness or bump to the head that started all this, I can’t say there’s been one.\n\nDoctor: How long have these experiences been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about nine months now, give or take. Not every single day, no, but on and off. That’s why at first I didn’t make too much of it, because one week it might happen a few times and then it would quiet down some, and I’d think maybe it was nothing.\n\nBut when I look back on it, the voices and those little messages in things people say or on the radio… that’s been going on close to nine months. Long enough that my wife noticed I was getting bothered by it, and long enough that I can’t really just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. At my age, I suppose I do have those moments where I walk into a room and forget what I went in there for, or I’ll misplace my glasses and then find them somewhere silly. But I can’t say I’ve noticed some big change, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes my wife will say, “You already told me that,” and I’ll laugh it off, because I’ve been telling long stories my whole life, so maybe I just repeat myself. But I still know who people are, I know where I am, I keep up with the day well enough most of the time. So… I’m not sure I’d call it trouble with my memory. Maybe just ordinary old-man forgetfulness, unless you mean something more particular.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed that your memory or thinking has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Well… she hasn’t sat me down and said, “Your memory is worse,” not in so many words. She’ll notice little things, sure — like if I tell her the same story twice, or if I put something down and then fuss around looking for it. But she’s been married to me a long time, so she also knows I’ve always been a bit that way, especially with my things.\n\nAs for my thinking, I don’t know. I suppose she might say I get more stuck on something than I used to, or I take longer to sort things out, but I’m not sure if that’s my memory getting worse or just me being old and tired some days. She hasn’t told me, “This past year you’ve really gone downhill,” nothing like that that I can remember. If you asked her, she might have her own opinion, but from what she’s said to me, I don’t think there’s been some clear big change.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have everyday tasks become harder for you than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Some things have, yes. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always kept myself busy, even after I wasn’t working anymore. But I’ve missed choir rehearsals, and that’s not like me. I used to be steady with that — you know, show up, sit where I sit, sing my part, see the same folks. Lately I’ll think about going and then I just… don’t. Or I get there in my mind, if that makes sense, but not out the door.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I’ve always taken pride in that little patch. Even if it wasn’t perfect, I’d keep after it. But there are parts of it I’ve let go, weeds coming up where I would’ve never tolerated it before. I see it, and I know it needs doing, but it feels harder to get myself to stay with it.\n\nThe domino games are maybe the most embarrassing. I used to enjoy that, sitting with the fellas, joking around. But I guess I’ve made it uncomfortable sometimes, or I feel like I have, and now I don’t join in like I used to. So yes, everyday things haven’t stopped completely, but they’ve gotten harder in those ways.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you ever had anything like this earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No… not really like this, not that I can think of. I mean, I’ve had hard times in my life, like anybody who gets to be my age, but the voices and those special messages — that’s been more recent. On and off, I’d say about nine months now.\n\nIt’s not every minute of every day, but it’s been around enough that I can’t say it’s just one odd afternoon or something I imagined once and forgot about. Before that, I don’t remember having this same kind of thing earlier in my life.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife had to start handling things for you that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly in the sense that she’s taken over all my affairs or anything like that. I still try to do my own things. But I suppose, if I’m being honest, she’s had to do more nudging and reminding than she used to.\n\nLike with choir — I used to keep track of that myself. I knew what night it was, I’d get ready, I’d go. Lately I’ve missed rehearsals, and she’ll say, “Aren’t you supposed to be going tonight?” or she’ll notice when I don’t. And the garden, that was always my thing. She didn’t have to tell me about weeds or which part needed attention, but now there are sections I’ve just let go, and she’ll point it out or try to encourage me to get back to it.\n\nAnd the domino games… she doesn’t handle that for me, exactly, but she knows I don’t go like I used to. I think I’ve made things uncomfortable there, or at least it feels that way, and I’ve pulled back. So I wouldn’t say she’s taken over my life, no. But yes, she’s had to notice more, remind me more, and sort of push me along in places I used to manage without anyone saying a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you been told by a doctor that you have Parkinson’s disease?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe any doctor has told me that. Parkinson’s, you mean like that specific disease? No, I don’t recall ever being given that diagnosis. I’ve seen doctors over the years for this and that, the usual things when you get older, but nobody has sat me down and said, “You have Parkinson’s disease,” not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people don’t seem to see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… no, not really seeing, I don’t think. It’s more hearing, if I’m saying it right.\n\nSome afternoons, when the house is quiet — and it gets very quiet in there, you know, when there’s no television on and my wife is in another room or out doing something — I’ll hear my old friend call my name from the porch. Clear as anything. Not like a memory exactly, not like I’m just thinking of him. It’ll sound like he’s standing out there and saying my name, like he used to.\n\nAnd sometimes I hear him hum one of the choir songs, just a little bit of it, but clear enough that I know the tune. I’ll stop what I’m doing and listen, because for a second it feels so real. Then I’ll look, or I’ll wait, and there’s nobody there. So… seeing, no. But hearing, yes, that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems with your hearing?\n\nPatient: My hearing? I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really thought of myself as hard of hearing, if that’s what you mean. My wife might say I don’t always catch what she says, but sometimes she’s talking from the other room, and I tell her nobody can hear properly through walls. \n\nI don’t wear hearing aids or anything like that. I can usually follow a conversation if someone’s in front of me and speaking clear. In a noisy place, maybe it gets muddled, but I think that happens to a lot of people my age. So… no major problem that I know of. Not something a doctor has told me about, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain condition, like a stroke or dementia?\n\nPatient: A brain condition… no, I don’t think I’ve been told that. Not a stroke, no. Nobody said dementia to me either, at least not in any official way. I’ve had doctors ask me questions, you know, what day it is and who the president is, that kind of thing, but I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying, “You’ve had a stroke,” or “You have dementia.”\n\nAt my age, people forget names or misplace things now and then, and sometimes folks make a big deal out of it, but as far as a diagnosis like that, no. Not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medicines in the past year?\n\nPatient: New medicines… hmm. I’m trying to think back over the year, and that’s harder than it sounds, because the months sort of run together when you’re not working and the routine is mostly the same.\n\nI don’t believe so. Not that I can clearly remember. I take what I’m supposed to take, and my wife helps keep an eye on things, but I don’t recall a doctor saying, “Here, this is a brand-new medicine,” recently. There may have been refills, or the pharmacy giving me bottles that look a little different, but as far as something new started in the past year… I’m not sure. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times in the past year when you felt confused or not clearheaded?\n\nPatient: Confused or not clearheaded… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are mornings where I wake up and it takes me a minute to get myself going, remember what day it is or what I was planning to do, but I don’t know if I’d call that confusion. Sometimes if there’s a lot going on, or people are talking fast, my head feels kind of cloudy, like I need folks to slow down.\n\nBut as far as big spells where I didn’t know where I was or couldn’t make sense of things, I can’t really say that’s happened. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. My wife might have her own opinion, because wives notice everything, but from my side, I’d say nothing too clear-cut. More just ordinary getting older and needing a little time to think.\n\nDoctor: Because of these experiences, have you stopped managing any of your usual responsibilities on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say I stopped managing everything, no. I still try to do my part around the house and keep up with things, but there are some things I’ve let slide, if I’m being honest.\n\nLike choir, for example. I used to be pretty steady about going to rehearsals, and lately I’ve missed some. Sometimes I just don’t get myself together to go, or by the time it’s time to leave, I’ve sort of talked myself out of it. And my garden too — that used to be something I kept up with better. I knew what needed watering, what needed trimming, what weeds to pull. Now there are parts of it I just let go longer than I should, and then it feels like too much to catch back up.\n\nAnd the domino games… I used to enjoy those. But I think I’ve made them uncomfortable a few times, or maybe I just felt like people weren’t enjoying me being there the same way. So I don’t join like I used to. I still think about going, but then I don’t. So yes, I guess some of my regular responsibilities and activities, I haven’t been keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

---------- turn 17 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type). You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: Doctor, I came because the men at the domino table and sometimes the radio announcer give little messages meant for me, and even when my wife says it is just talk, I know they are warning me to be careful.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Some afternoons, when the house is quiet, I hear my old friend call my name from the porch or hum a choir song, clear like he is standing right there.\n- key: S022\n  name: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\n  description: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: At domino games I have started description: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: I still love my family, but many days I just sit ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Clearheaded… I’m not sure. That’s a hard one, because in the moment it can feel like I’m clear enough, you know? Like I’m sitting there thinking, “Okay, I’m understanding what’s happening,” but then later, when I try to explain it, I don’t always know how clear it really was.

I don’t think I feel drunk or completely out of it, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually aware of where I am and what I’m doing. But my mind can get kind of… occupied, I guess. Like I’m paying so much attention to it that other things don’t feel as sharp. It’s not like regular thinking, exactly, but I don’t know if I’d call it confusion either.

So I guess my honest answer is: I’m not really sure. Sometimes I feel clearheaded enough at the time, but I don’t always trust my own judgment about it afterward.

Maybe just ordinary old-man forgetfulness, unless you mean something more particular.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed that your memory or thinking has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Well… she hasn’t sat me down and said, “Your memory is worse,” not in so many words. She’ll notice little things, sure — like if I tell her the same story twice, or if I put something down and then fuss around looking for it. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always kept myself busy, even after I wasn’t working anymore. But I’ve missed choir rehearsals, and that’s not like me. I used to be steady with that — you know, show up, sit where I sit, sing my part, see the same folks. Lately I’ll think about going and then I just… don’t. Or I get there in my mind, if that makes sense, but not out the door.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I’ve always taken pride in that little patch. Even if it wasn’t perfect, I’d keep after it. But there are parts of it I’ve let go, weeds coming up where I would’ve never tolerated it before. I see it, and I know it needs doing, but it feels harder to get myself to stay with it.\n\nThe domino games are maybe the most embarrassing. I used to enjoy that, sitting with the fellas, joking around. But I guess I’ve made it uncomfortable sometimes, or I feel like I have, and now I don’t join in like I used to. So yes, everyday things haven’t stopped completely, but they’ve gotten harder in those ways.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you ever had anything like this earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No… not really like this, not that I can think of. I mean, I’ve had hard times in my life, like anybody who gets to be my age, but the voices and those special messages — that’s been more recent. On and off, I’d say about nine months now.\n\nIt’s not every minute of every day, but it’s been around enough that I can’t say it’s just one odd afternoon or something I imagined once and forgot about. Before that, I don’t remember having this same kind of thing earlier in my life.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife had to start handling things for you that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly in the sense that she’s taken over all my affairs or anything like that. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because… it’s been bothering me, and my wife said I ought to talk to somebody, though she doesn’t see it the way I do. When I’m sitting with the men at the domino table, you know, just regular talk going on, sometimes I can tell there are little messages in what they’re saying. Not loud, not like they come right out and say my name, but the way they say certain things, or repeat something, it feels meant for me.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s the radio announcer too. I’ll be listening, minding my own business, and there’ll be a phrase or a warning kind of tucked in there, like they’re telling me to be careful. My wife says, “That’s just talk, that’s just the radio,” but I don’t think it’s just nothing. I know when something is meant for me. It’s got me uneasy, and that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear a voice speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do sometimes. Not all day or anything like that, and it’s not usually when there’s a lot going on. It’s more in the afternoon, when the house gets real quiet and I’m sitting there by myself. I’ll hear my old friend call my name, like from out on the porch. Clear enough that I’ll turn my head, because it sounds like he’s standing right there.\n\nOther times it’s not words exactly, it’ll be him humming an old choir song, the kind we used to hear years back. And I know how that sounds, doctor, I know folks might say it’s just my mind or my ears playing tricks, but in the moment it feels real plain to me. Like someone is right there just outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: Well… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. I haven’t had any big fall where I hit my head or anything like that, at least nothing I’d call a head injury. And I haven’t been laid up sick recently either, not in some serious way.\n\nI’m 84, so, you know, there’s always little aches and tired days and such, and sometimes I have to think a minute because things run together when you get older. But as far as some recent illness or bump to the head that started all this, I can’t say there’s been one.\n\nDoctor: How long have these experiences been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about nine months now, give or take. Not every single day, no, but on and off. That’s why at first I didn’t make too much of it, because one week it might happen a few times and then it would quiet down some, and I’d think maybe it was nothing.\n\nBut when I look back on it, the voices and those little messages in things people say or on the radio… that’s been going on close to nine months. Long enough that my wife noticed I was getting bothered by it, and long enough that I can’t really just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. At my age, I suppose I do have those moments where I walk into a room and forget what I went in there for, or I’ll misplace my glasses and then find them somewhere silly. But I can’t say I’ve noticed some big change, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes my wife will say, “You already told me that,” and I’ll laugh it off, because I’ve been telling long stories my whole life, so maybe I just repeat myself. But I still know who people are, I know where I am, I keep up with the day well enough most of the time. So… I’m not sure I’d call it trouble with my memory. Maybe just ordinary old-man forgetfulness, unless you mean something more particular.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed that your memory or thinking has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Well… she hasn’t sat me down and said, “Your memory is worse,” not in so many words. She’ll notice little things, sure — like if I tell her the same story twice, or if I put something down and then fuss around looking for it. But she’s been married to me a long time, so she also knows I’ve always been a bit that way, especially with my things.\n\nAs for my thinking, I don’t know. I suppose she might say I get more stuck on something than I used to, or I take longer to sort things out, but I’m not sure if that’s my memory getting worse or just me being old and tired some days. She hasn’t told me, “This past year you’ve really gone downhill,” nothing like that that I can remember. If you asked her, she might have her own opinion, but from what she’s said to me, I don’t think there’s been some clear big change.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have everyday tasks become harder for you than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Some things have, yes. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always kept myself busy, even after I wasn’t working anymore. But I’ve missed choir rehearsals, and that’s not like me. I used to be steady with that — you know, show up, sit where I sit, sing my part, see the same folks. Lately I’ll think about going and then I just… don’t. Or I get there in my mind, if that makes sense, but not out the door.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I’ve always taken pride in that little patch. Even if it wasn’t perfect, I’d keep after it. But there are parts of it I’ve let go, weeds coming up where I would’ve never tolerated it before. I see it, and I know it needs doing, but it feels harder to get myself to stay with it.\n\nThe domino games are maybe the most embarrassing. I used to enjoy that, sitting with the fellas, joking around. But I guess I’ve made it uncomfortable sometimes, or I feel like I have, and now I don’t join in like I used to. So yes, everyday things haven’t stopped completely, but they’ve gotten harder in those ways.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you ever had anything like this earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No… not really like this, not that I can think of. I mean, I’ve had hard times in my life, like anybody who gets to be my age, but the voices and those special messages — that’s been more recent. On and off, I’d say about nine months now.\n\nIt’s not every minute of every day, but it’s been around enough that I can’t say it’s just one odd afternoon or something I imagined once and forgot about. Before that, I don’t remember having this same kind of thing earlier in my life.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife had to start handling things for you that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly in the sense that she’s taken over all my affairs or anything like that. I still try to do my own things. But I suppose, if I’m being honest, she’s had to do more nudging and reminding than she used to.\n\nLike with choir — I used to keep track of that myself. I knew what night it was, I’d get ready, I’d go. Lately I’ve missed rehearsals, and she’ll say, “Aren’t you supposed to be going tonight?” or she’ll notice when I don’t. And the garden, that was always my thing. She didn’t have to tell me about weeds or which part needed attention, but now there are sections I’ve just let go, and she’ll point it out or try to encourage me to get back to it.\n\nAnd the domino games… she doesn’t handle that for me, exactly, but she knows I don’t go like I used to. I think I’ve made things uncomfortable there, or at least it feels that way, and I’ve pulled back. So I wouldn’t say she’s taken over my life, no. But yes, she’s had to notice more, remind me more, and sort of push me along in places I used to manage without anyone saying a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you been told by a doctor that you have Parkinson’s disease?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe any doctor has told me that. Parkinson’s, you mean like that specific disease? No, I don’t recall ever being given that diagnosis. I’ve seen doctors over the years for this and that, the usual things when you get older, but nobody has sat me down and said, “You have Parkinson’s disease,” not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people don’t seem to see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… no, not really seeing, I don’t think. It’s more hearing, if I’m saying it right.\n\nSome afternoons, when the house is quiet — and it gets very quiet in there, you know, when there’s no television on and my wife is in another room or out doing something — I’ll hear my old friend call my name from the porch. Clear as anything. Not like a memory exactly, not like I’m just thinking of him. It’ll sound like he’s standing out there and saying my name, like he used to.\n\nAnd sometimes I hear him hum one of the choir songs, just a little bit of it, but clear enough that I know the tune. I’ll stop what I’m doing and listen, because for a second it feels so real. Then I’ll look, or I’ll wait, and there’s nobody there. So… seeing, no. But hearing, yes, that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems with your hearing?\n\nPatient: My hearing? I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really thought of myself as hard of hearing, if that’s what you mean. My wife might say I don’t always catch what she says, but sometimes she’s talking from the other room, and I tell her nobody can hear properly through walls. \n\nI don’t wear hearing aids or anything like that. I can usually follow a conversation if someone’s in front of me and speaking clear. In a noisy place, maybe it gets muddled, but I think that happens to a lot of people my age. So… no major problem that I know of. Not something a doctor has told me about, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain condition, like a stroke or dementia?\n\nPatient: A brain condition… no, I don’t think I’ve been told that. Not a stroke, no. Nobody said dementia to me either, at least not in any official way. I’ve had doctors ask me questions, you know, what day it is and who the president is, that kind of thing, but I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying, “You’ve had a stroke,” or “You have dementia.”\n\nAt my age, people forget names or misplace things now and then, and sometimes folks make a big deal out of it, but as far as a diagnosis like that, no. Not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medicines in the past year?\n\nPatient: New medicines… hmm. I’m trying to think back over the year, and that’s harder than it sounds, because the months sort of run together when you’re not working and the routine is mostly the same.\n\nI don’t believe so. Not that I can clearly remember. I take what I’m supposed to take, and my wife helps keep an eye on things, but I don’t recall a doctor saying, “Here, this is a brand-new medicine,” recently. There may have been refills, or the pharmacy giving me bottles that look a little different, but as far as something new started in the past year… I’m not sure. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times in the past year when you felt confused or not clearheaded?\n\nPatient: Confused or not clearheaded… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are mornings where I wake up and it takes me a minute to get myself going, remember what day it is or what I was planning to do, but I don’t know if I’d call that confusion. Sometimes if there’s a lot going on, or people are talking fast, my head feels kind of cloudy, like I need folks to slow down.\n\nBut as far as big spells where I didn’t know where I was or couldn’t make sense of things, I can’t really say that’s happened. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. My wife might have her own opinion, because wives notice everything, but from my side, I’d say nothing too clear-cut. More just ordinary getting older and needing a little time to think.\n\nDoctor: Because of these experiences, have you stopped managing any of your usual responsibilities on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say I stopped managing everything, no. I still try to do my part around the house and keep up with things, but there are some things I’ve let slide, if I’m being honest.\n\nLike choir, for example. I used to be pretty steady about going to rehearsals, and lately I’ve missed some. Sometimes I just don’t get myself together to go, or by the time it’s time to leave, I’ve sort of talked myself out of it. And my garden too — that used to be something I kept up with better. I knew what needed watering, what needed trimming, what weeds to pull. Now there are parts of it I just let go longer than I should, and then it feels like too much to catch back up.\n\nAnd the domino games… I used to enjoy those. But I think I’ve made them uncomfortable a few times, or maybe I just felt like people weren’t enjoying me being there the same way. So I don’t join like I used to. I still think about going, but then I don’t. So yes, I guess some of my regular responsibilities and activities, I haven’t been keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: When these voices or messages happen, do you feel clearheaded?\n\nPatient: Clearheaded… I’m not sure. That’s a hard one, because in the moment it can feel like I’m clear enough, you know? Like I’m sitting there thinking, “Okay, I’m understanding what’s happening,” but then later, when I try to explain it, I don’t always know how clear it really was.\n\nI don’t think I feel drunk or completely out of it, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually aware of where I am and what I’m doing. But my mind can get kind of… occupied, I guess. Like I’m paying so much attention to it that other things don’t feel as sharp. It’s not like regular thinking, exactly, but I don’t know if I’d call it confusion either.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I’m not really sure. Sometimes I feel clearheaded enough at the time, but I don’t always trust my own judgment about it afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A tremor… you mean like my hands shaking? I don’t think so, not in any big way that I’ve noticed.

Sometimes if I’m tired or I’m trying to do something small, like buttoning a shirt or holding a cup just right, I might feel like my hands aren’t as steady as when I was younger, but I always figured that was just age or being a little worn out. I haven’t had, you know, a constant shaking where I sit there and watch my hand move on its own. At least not that I can think of.

My wife might notice little things before I do, because she pays attention to everything, but from my side, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a tremor.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=gpt-5.4 ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.2, F20.0, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came in because… it’s been bothering me, and my wife said I ought to talk to somebody, though she doesn’t see it the way I do. When I’m sitting with the men at the domino table, you know, just regular talk going on, sometimes I can tell there are little messages in what they’re saying. Not loud, not like they come right out and say my name, but the way they say certain things, or repeat something, it feels meant for me.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s the radio announcer too. I’ll be listening, minding my own business, and there’ll be a phrase or a warning kind of tucked in there, like they’re telling me to be careful. My wife says, “That’s just talk, that’s just the radio,” but I don’t think it’s just nothing. I know when something is meant for me. It’s got me uneasy, and that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear a voice speaking when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do sometimes. Not all day or anything like that, and it’s not usually when there’s a lot going on. It’s more in the afternoon, when the house gets real quiet and I’m sitting there by myself. I’ll hear my old friend call my name, like from out on the porch. Clear enough that I’ll turn my head, because it sounds like he’s standing right there.\n\nOther times it’s not words exactly, it’ll be him humming an old choir song, the kind we used to hear years back. And I know how that sounds, doctor, I know folks might say it’s just my mind or my ears playing tricks, but in the moment it feels real plain to me. Like someone is right there just outside.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: Well… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can point to. I haven’t had any big fall where I hit my head or anything like that, at least nothing I’d call a head injury. And I haven’t been laid up sick recently either, not in some serious way.\n\nI’m 84, so, you know, there’s always little aches and tired days and such, and sometimes I have to think a minute because things run together when you get older. But as far as some recent illness or bump to the head that started all this, I can’t say there’s been one.\n\nDoctor: How long have these experiences been going on?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about nine months now, give or take. Not every single day, no, but on and off. That’s why at first I didn’t make too much of it, because one week it might happen a few times and then it would quiet down some, and I’d think maybe it was nothing.\n\nBut when I look back on it, the voices and those little messages in things people say or on the radio… that’s been going on close to nine months. Long enough that my wife noticed I was getting bothered by it, and long enough that I can’t really just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory… well, I don’t know. At my age, I suppose I do have those moments where I walk into a room and forget what I went in there for, or I’ll misplace my glasses and then find them somewhere silly. But I can’t say I’ve noticed some big change, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes my wife will say, “You already told me that,” and I’ll laugh it off, because I’ve been telling long stories my whole life, so maybe I just repeat myself. But I still know who people are, I know where I am, I keep up with the day well enough most of the time. So… I’m not sure I’d call it trouble with my memory. Maybe just ordinary old-man forgetfulness, unless you mean something more particular.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed that your memory or thinking has gotten worse over the past year?\n\nPatient: Well… she hasn’t sat me down and said, “Your memory is worse,” not in so many words. She’ll notice little things, sure — like if I tell her the same story twice, or if I put something down and then fuss around looking for it. But she’s been married to me a long time, so she also knows I’ve always been a bit that way, especially with my things.\n\nAs for my thinking, I don’t know. I suppose she might say I get more stuck on something than I used to, or I take longer to sort things out, but I’m not sure if that’s my memory getting worse or just me being old and tired some days. She hasn’t told me, “This past year you’ve really gone downhill,” nothing like that that I can remember. If you asked her, she might have her own opinion, but from what she’s said to me, I don’t think there’s been some clear big change.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have everyday tasks become harder for you than they used to be?\n\nPatient: Some things have, yes. I don’t like admitting that, because I’ve always kept myself busy, even after I wasn’t working anymore. But I’ve missed choir rehearsals, and that’s not like me. I used to be steady with that — you know, show up, sit where I sit, sing my part, see the same folks. Lately I’ll think about going and then I just… don’t. Or I get there in my mind, if that makes sense, but not out the door.\n\nAnd my garden, too. I’ve always taken pride in that little patch. Even if it wasn’t perfect, I’d keep after it. But there are parts of it I’ve let go, weeds coming up where I would’ve never tolerated it before. I see it, and I know it needs doing, but it feels harder to get myself to stay with it.\n\nThe domino games are maybe the most embarrassing. I used to enjoy that, sitting with the fellas, joking around. But I guess I’ve made it uncomfortable sometimes, or I feel like I have, and now I don’t join in like I used to. So yes, everyday things haven’t stopped completely, but they’ve gotten harder in those ways.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you ever had anything like this earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: No… not really like this, not that I can think of. I mean, I’ve had hard times in my life, like anybody who gets to be my age, but the voices and those special messages — that’s been more recent. On and off, I’d say about nine months now.\n\nIt’s not every minute of every day, but it’s been around enough that I can’t say it’s just one odd afternoon or something I imagined once and forgot about. Before that, I don’t remember having this same kind of thing earlier in my life.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife had to start handling things for you that you used to manage on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… not exactly in the sense that she’s taken over all my affairs or anything like that. I still try to do my own things. But I suppose, if I’m being honest, she’s had to do more nudging and reminding than she used to.\n\nLike with choir — I used to keep track of that myself. I knew what night it was, I’d get ready, I’d go. Lately I’ve missed rehearsals, and she’ll say, “Aren’t you supposed to be going tonight?” or she’ll notice when I don’t. And the garden, that was always my thing. She didn’t have to tell me about weeds or which part needed attention, but now there are sections I’ve just let go, and she’ll point it out or try to encourage me to get back to it.\n\nAnd the domino games… she doesn’t handle that for me, exactly, but she knows I don’t go like I used to. I think I’ve made things uncomfortable there, or at least it feels that way, and I’ve pulled back. So I wouldn’t say she’s taken over my life, no. But yes, she’s had to notice more, remind me more, and sort of push me along in places I used to manage without anyone saying a word.\n\nDoctor: Have you been told by a doctor that you have Parkinson’s disease?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe any doctor has told me that. Parkinson’s, you mean like that specific disease? No, I don’t recall ever being given that diagnosis. I’ve seen doctors over the years for this and that, the usual things when you get older, but nobody has sat me down and said, “You have Parkinson’s disease,” not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people don’t seem to see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… no, not really seeing, I don’t think. It’s more hearing, if I’m saying it right.\n\nSome afternoons, when the house is quiet — and it gets very quiet in there, you know, when there’s no television on and my wife is in another room or out doing something — I’ll hear my old friend call my name from the porch. Clear as anything. Not like a memory exactly, not like I’m just thinking of him. It’ll sound like he’s standing out there and saying my name, like he used to.\n\nAnd sometimes I hear him hum one of the choir songs, just a little bit of it, but clear enough that I know the tune. I’ll stop what I’m doing and listen, because for a second it feels so real. Then I’ll look, or I’ll wait, and there’s nobody there. So… seeing, no. But hearing, yes, that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any problems with your hearing?\n\nPatient: My hearing? I don’t know… I mean, I haven’t really thought of myself as hard of hearing, if that’s what you mean. My wife might say I don’t always catch what she says, but sometimes she’s talking from the other room, and I tell her nobody can hear properly through walls. \n\nI don’t wear hearing aids or anything like that. I can usually follow a conversation if someone’s in front of me and speaking clear. In a noisy place, maybe it gets muddled, but I think that happens to a lot of people my age. So… no major problem that I know of. Not something a doctor has told me about, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain condition, like a stroke or dementia?\n\nPatient: A brain condition… no, I don’t think I’ve been told that. Not a stroke, no. Nobody said dementia to me either, at least not in any official way. I’ve had doctors ask me questions, you know, what day it is and who the president is, that kind of thing, but I don’t recall anyone sitting me down and saying, “You’ve had a stroke,” or “You have dementia.”\n\nAt my age, people forget names or misplace things now and then, and sometimes folks make a big deal out of it, but as far as a diagnosis like that, no. Not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medicines in the past year?\n\nPatient: New medicines… hmm. I’m trying to think back over the year, and that’s harder than it sounds, because the months sort of run together when you’re not working and the routine is mostly the same.\n\nI don’t believe so. Not that I can clearly remember. I take what I’m supposed to take, and my wife helps keep an eye on things, but I don’t recall a doctor saying, “Here, this is a brand-new medicine,” recently. There may have been refills, or the pharmacy giving me bottles that look a little different, but as far as something new started in the past year… I’m not sure. I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times in the past year when you felt confused or not clearheaded?\n\nPatient: Confused or not clearheaded… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, there are mornings where I wake up and it takes me a minute to get myself going, remember what day it is or what I was planning to do, but I don’t know if I’d call that confusion. Sometimes if there’s a lot going on, or people are talking fast, my head feels kind of cloudy, like I need folks to slow down.\n\nBut as far as big spells where I didn’t know where I was or couldn’t make sense of things, I can’t really say that’s happened. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. My wife might have her own opinion, because wives notice everything, but from my side, I’d say nothing too clear-cut. More just ordinary getting older and needing a little time to think.\n\nDoctor: Because of these experiences, have you stopped managing any of your usual responsibilities on your own?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say I stopped managing everything, no. I still try to do my part around the house and keep up with things, but there are some things I’ve let slide, if I’m being honest.\n\nLike choir, for example. I used to be pretty steady about going to rehearsals, and lately I’ve missed some. Sometimes I just don’t get myself together to go, or by the time it’s time to leave, I’ve sort of talked myself out of it. And my garden too — that used to be something I kept up with better. I knew what needed watering, what needed trimming, what weeds to pull. Now there are parts of it I just let go longer than I should, and then it feels like too much to catch back up.\n\nAnd the domino games… I used to enjoy those. But I think I’ve made them uncomfortable a few times, or maybe I just felt like people weren’t enjoying me being there the same way. So I don’t join like I used to. I still think about going, but then I don’t. So yes, I guess some of my regular responsibilities and activities, I haven’t been keeping up with them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: When these voices or messages happen, do you feel clearheaded?\n\nPatient: Clearheaded… I’m not sure. That’s a hard one, because in the moment it can feel like I’m clear enough, you know? Like I’m sitting there thinking, “Okay, I’m understanding what’s happening,” but then later, when I try to explain it, I don’t always know how clear it really was.\n\nI don’t think I feel drunk or completely out of it, if that’s what you mean. I’m usually aware of where I am and what I’m doing. But my mind can get kind of… occupied, I guess. Like I’m paying so much attention to it that other things don’t feel as sharp. It’s not like regular thinking, exactly, but I don’t know if I’d call it confusion either.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I’m not really sure. Sometimes I feel clearheaded enough at the time, but I don’t always trust my own judgment about it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a tremor?\n\nPatient: A tremor… you mean like my hands shaking? I don’t think so, not in any big way that I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes if I’m tired or I’m trying to do something small, like buttoning a shirt or holding a cup just right, I might feel like my hands aren’t as steady as when I was younger, but I always figured that was just age or being a little worn out. I haven’t had, you know, a constant shaking where I sit there and watch my hand move on its own. At least not that I can think of.\n\nMy wife might notice little things before I do, because she pays attention to everything, but from my side, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a tremor.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates":["F06.2","F20.0","F22"],"note":"","is_final":false}

I don’t know that I’d say steadily worse, no. I mean, I’m 84, so I have those moments where I walk into a room and have to stand there a second thinking, “Now what did I come in here for?” Or I might misplace something and then find it in a perfectly sensible place later. Some days I’m sharper than other days, depending on how I slept or how much is on my mind, but I can still follow things most of the time.

So… no, not steadily worse that I’ve noticed. At least I wouldn’t describe it that way.

Not loud, not like they come right out and say my name, but the way they say certain things, or repeat something, it feels meant for me.\n\nAnd sometimes it’s the radio announcer too. I’ll be listening, minding my own business, and there’ll be a phrase or a warning kind of tucked in there, like they’re telling me to be careful.
